\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}(O)=\sum_{\psi}\braket{\psi,\uparrow|O|\psi,\downarrow},

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\uparrow}(O)=\sum_{\psi}\braket{\psi,\downarrow|O|\psi,\uparrow}.

(9.A.10)

This works because
\braket{\uparrow|\downarrow}=\braket{\downarrow|c_{0}b_{0}|\uparrow}=\braket{\downarrow|\uparrow}
does not vanish, so we may normalize the vacua such that
\braket{\uparrow|\downarrow}=1
. We wish do use these formulas to compute the trace of
(-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}b_{22}(\sigma)\,c^{2}(\hat{\sigma})
, using the mode expansions

\displaystyle b_{22}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{-n\sigma^{2}}\cos(n\sigma^{1})

\displaystyle c^{2}(\hat{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}c_{n}e^{-n\hat{\sigma}^{2}}\cos(n\hat{\sigma}^{1})

(9.A.11)

from Section
5
. To do this it is convenient to first derive some auxiliary results. First, note that the zero mode algebra (
9.A.5
) leads to
\text{Tr}_{\uparrow}(O)=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}(b_{0}Oc_{0})
, and therefore

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)+\text{Tr}_{\uparrow}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)-\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}b_{0}Oc_{0}\big)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)-\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}O\,b_{0}c_{0}\big)-\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}[b_{0},O]c_{0}\big).

(9.A.12)

We use
b_{0}c_{0}=-c_{0}b_{0}+1
on the second term and anticommute
b_{0}
to the right, past all creation operators, until it annihilates
\ket{\downarrow}
. The result is

\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}O\big)=-\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}[b_{0},O]c_{0}\big).

(9.A.13)

The mode expansions lead to a sum of terms of the form

\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}\,b_{m}c_{n}\big).

(9.A.14)

Let
A
be some bosonic operator that commutes with
b_{0}
and
c_{0}
(as is the case of
e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}
). Plugging
O=A\,b_{m}c_{n}
into (
9.A.13
) and using the anticommutation relations for the modes leads to
\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}A\,b_{m}b_{n}\big)=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}A\,b_{m}c_{0}\big)\delta_{n0}
. If
m\neq 0
, one may anticommute
c_{0}
to the left past all modes until it annihilates
\bra{\uparrow}
. Therefore, only the zero modes survive:

\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}A\,b_{m}b_{n}\big)=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}A\,b_{0}c_{0}\big)\delta_{m0}\delta_{n0}=\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}A\big)\delta_{m0}\delta_{n0}.

(9.A.15)

This leads to

\text{Tr}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}b_{22}(\sigma)\,c^{2}(\hat{\sigma})\big)=2\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}\big).

(9.A.16)

No dependence on the worldsheet coordinates remains. We now proceed in a way analogous to the bosonic computation in Section
13
. First write

L_{0}^{g}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\big(b_{-n}c_{n}+c_{-n}b_{n}\big)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\big(\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n}+\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n}\big),

(9.A.17)

where
\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n}=b_{-n}c_{n}
and
\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n}=c_{-n}b_{n}
are operators that count the number of
c
and
b
excitations at the particular mode number
n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle[\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n},c_{-m}]=\delta_{nm}c_{-m},\hskip 14.22636pt[\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n},b_{-m}]=\delta_{nm}b_{-m},\hskip 14.22636pt[\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n},b_{-m}]

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n},c_{-m}]=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 29.87547pt(n,m\geq 0).

(9.A.18)

Since the modes are fermionic,
\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n}
and
\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n}
can only be 0 or 1. We have

\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}\big)=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{Tr}^{(n)}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tn(\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n}+\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n})}\big),

(9.A.19)

where
\text{Tr}^{(n)}_{\downarrow}
means that the trace is taken only inside the subspace of fixed mode number
n
. Each of these subspaces only has four states, which can be labelled by their
(\mathbf{N}^{c}_{n},\mathbf{N}^{b}_{n})
eigenvalues. They are
(0,0)
,
(1,0)
,
(0,1)
and
(1,1)
. The first and last contain an even number of modes and therefore have
(-1)^{F}=1
, whereas the second and third have an odd number of modes, so they have
(-1)^{F}=-1
. This results in

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\downarrow}\big((-1)^{F}e^{-2\pi tL_{0}^{g}}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(e^{0}-e^{-2\pi tn}-e^{-2\pi tn}+e^{-4\pi tn}\big)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(1-e^{-2\pi tn}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\pi t}{6}}\eta(it)^{2},

(9.A.20)

where
\eta(x)
is defined in (
13.12
). Plugging this into (
9.A.16
) and that into (
9.A.4
) finally gives us the result

\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{open}}(t)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}t}e^{-2\pi ta^{g}}\int d^{2}\sigma\,e^{\frac{\pi t}{6}}\,\eta(it)^{2}=e^{-2\pi t\big(a^{g}-\frac{1}{12}\big)}\,\eta(it)^{2}.

(9.A.21)

Appendix 9.B
Closed string computation

The ghost contribution to the closed string amplitude of Section
14
is

\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi s}\int d^{2}\xi\int\mathcal{D}b\,\mathcal{D}c\,e^{-S_{g}[b,c]}\,b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi}),

(9.B.1)

where the
d^{2}\xi
integral is over a cylinder defined by
[0,2\pi]\times[0,s]
and the path integral over the ghosts is understood to interpolate between boundary states which impose the open string boundary conditions
b_{12}=c^{2}=0
at
\xi^{2}=0,s
. In operator language we have

\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi s}\int d^{2}\xi\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\,\braket{B|e^{-s(L^{g}_{0}+\tilde{L}^{g}_{0}+2a^{g})}\,e^{i\theta(L^{g}_{0}-\tilde{L}^{g}_{0})}\,b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi})|B},

(9.B.2)

where the ghost boundary state
\ket{B}
satisfies

b_{12}(\xi^{1},0)\ket{B}=c^{2}(\xi^{1},0)\ket{B}=0.

(9.B.3)

The mode expansions of the closed string ghosts in Euclidean signature are

\displaystyle b_{11}(\xi)

\displaystyle=-b_{22}(\xi)=\sum_{n}\Big(b_{n}e^{in(\xi^{1}+i\xi^{2})}+\tilde{b}_{n}e^{-in(\xi^{1}-i\xi^{2})}\Big)

\displaystyle b_{12}(\xi)

\displaystyle=b_{21}(\xi)=i\sum_{n}\Big(b_{n}e^{in(\xi^{1}+i\xi^{2})}-\tilde{b}_{n}e^{-in(\xi^{1}-i\xi^{2})}\Big)

\displaystyle c^{1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\Big(c_{n}e^{in(\xi^{1}+i\xi^{2})}-\tilde{c}_{n}e^{-in(\xi^{1}-i\xi^{2})}\Big)

\displaystyle c^{2}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n}\Big(c_{n}e^{in(\xi^{1}+i\xi^{2})}+\tilde{c}_{n}e^{-in(\xi^{1}-i\xi^{2})}\Big).

(9.B.4)

In terms of these the conditions (
9.B.3
) become

\displaystyle(b_{n}-\tilde{b}_{-n})\ket{B}=(c_{n}+\tilde{c}_{-n})\ket{B}=0.

(9.B.5)

We can solve for
\ket{B}
using the trick explained in footnote
46
, adapted for fermionic variables. Fix some
n>0
. The algebraic relations
\{b_{n},c_{-n}\}=\{\tilde{b}_{n},\tilde{c}_{-n}\}=1
are preserved under the identifications

\displaystyle b_{-n}\to\theta,

\displaystyle c_{n}\to\partial_{\theta},

\displaystyle c_{-n}\to\epsilon,

\displaystyle b_{n}\to\partial_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{-n}\to\tilde{\theta},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{n}\to\partial_{\tilde{\theta}},

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{-n}\to\tilde{\epsilon},

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{n}\to\partial_{\tilde{\epsilon}},

(9.B.6)

where
\theta
and
\epsilon
are Grassmann variables, with respect to which derivatives are to be taken from the left, meaning that
\partial_{\theta}(\theta\epsilon)=\epsilon
,
\partial_{\theta}(\epsilon\theta)=-\epsilon
. The boundary state
\ket{B}
is then identified with a function
f=f(\theta,\tilde{\theta},\epsilon,\tilde{\epsilon})
. Under these identifications the conditions (
9.B.5
) become the four differential equations

(\partial_{\theta}+\tilde{\epsilon})f=(\partial_{\epsilon}-\tilde{\theta})f=(\partial_{\tilde{\theta}}+\epsilon)f=(\partial_{\tilde{\epsilon}}-\theta)f=0,

(9.B.7)

which are solved by
f\sim e^{-(\theta\tilde{\epsilon}+\tilde{\theta}\epsilon)}
. This determines

\ket{B}=e^{-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}+\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n})}\ket{B_{0}},

(9.B.8)

where
\ket{B_{0}}
is the ghost vacuum part of
\ket{B}
, which solves
(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})\ket{B_{0}}=(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})\ket{B_{0}}=0
. Starting from the general ansatz

\ket{B_{0}}=(A+Bc_{0}+C\tilde{c}_{0}+Dc_{0}\tilde{c}_{0})\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}+(E+Fb_{0}+G\tilde{b}_{0}+Hb_{0}\tilde{b}_{0})\ket{\uparrow\uparrow},

(9.B.9)

the zero-mode constraints lead to
A=D=E=H=0
and
B=C
,
F=-G
, so the form of
\ket{B_{0}}
reduces to a combination of
(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}
and
(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})\ket{\uparrow\uparrow}
. For the purposes of computing
\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)
the precise values of the coefficients in this combination are not required, since they would only contribute to the overall prefactor in front of the amplitude, which cannot be determined from solving (
9.B.5
) for the boundary state anyway. Since the Virasoro generators
L_{0}^{g}
and
\tilde{L}_{0}^{g}
commute with all ghost zero modes and annihilate the vacua, we know that the part of
\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)
that involves the ghost vacua is proportional to
\braket{B_{0}|(b_{0}+\tilde{b}_{0})(c_{0}-\tilde{c}_{0})|B_{0}}
, with the zero-modes coming from
b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi})
. It is straightforward to check that due to these zero-modes one obtains a nonzero result only when the
(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})
part of either
\ket{B_{0}}
or
\bra{B_{0}}
hits the
(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})
part of the other. Because of this, it is consistent, up to an overall multiplicative term in the amplitude, to set
\ket{B_{0}}=(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}
and
\bra{B_{0}}=\bra{\uparrow\uparrow}(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})
.
75
75

75

A less ad hoc justification for these comes from a BRST analysis of the worldsheet theory, which requires one to consider the matter and ghost sectors simultaneously, not separately as we have done. One then finds that on-shell states must satisfy
b_{0}\ket{\psi}=\tilde{b}_{0}\ket{\psi}=0
(see Section 5.2 of
[
22
]
for a proof). These conditions eliminate all states built on top of the
\ket{\uparrow\uparrow}
vacuum from the theory. If one builds the boundary states starting from this constrained Hilbert space, the conditions (
9.B.5
) uniquely determine
\ket{B_{0}}=(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}
. The inner product structure show in footnote
74
means that the dual state
\bra{B_{0}}
should then be constructed over the
\bra{\uparrow\uparrow}
vacuum, which sets
\bra{B_{0}}=\bra{\uparrow\uparrow}(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})
.
The matrix element in
\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)
is therefore given by

\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})e^{b_{n}\tilde{c}_{n}}e^{\tilde{b}_{n}c_{n}}e^{-s(L_{0}^{g}+\tilde{L}_{0}^{g})}b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi})e^{-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}}e^{-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}}(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})|\downarrow\downarrow}.

(9.B.10)

We set the overall normalization of the boundary states to 1, since it can be absorbed into
N_{p}
, the normalization of the bosonic matter boundary states defined in (
14.18
). The
d\theta
integral in (
9.B.2
) is trivial because the boundary states satisfy
L_{0}^{g}=\tilde{L}^{g}_{0}
. Note that the
\ket{B_{0}}
vacuum has zero norm, in the sense that

\braket{B_{0}|B_{0}}=\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0}))|\downarrow\downarrow}=0.

(9.B.11)

It follows that any expression of the form
\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})O(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0}))|\downarrow\downarrow}
vanishes if
O
can be (anti-)commuted past either
(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})
or
(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})
. This is the case of all terms in the expansion of
b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi})
except for the one only containing the zero modes, so we can substitute

b_{22}(\xi)c^{1}(\hat{\xi})\to\frac{1}{2}(b_{0}+\tilde{b}_{0})(c_{0}-\tilde{c}_{0})

(9.B.12)

in (
9.B.10
). All dependence on the coordinates is then gone and the
d^{2}\xi
integral just gives the worldsheet area
\int d^{2}\xi=2\pi s
. We are left with

\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)=2e^{-2sa^{g}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|e^{b_{n}\tilde{c}_{n}}e^{\tilde{b}_{n}c_{n}}e^{-s(L_{0}^{g}+\tilde{L}_{0}^{g})}e^{-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}}e^{-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}}|\downarrow\downarrow}

(9.B.13)

after using the algebra to simplify the zero-mode terms.
76
76

76

One actually obtains minus this expression, due to the fact that

\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|(b_{0}-\tilde{b}_{0})(b_{0}+\tilde{b}_{0})(c_{0}-\tilde{c}_{0})(c_{0}+\tilde{c}_{0})|\downarrow\downarrow}=-4\braket{\uparrow\uparrow|\downarrow\downarrow}=-4.

(9.B.14)

However, the contribution of the ghosts to a path integral is only a way of representing the Faddeev-Popov determinant, which is a Jacobian factor that arises from changing variables, and therefore contributes with its absolute value. We should therefore discard any overall signs.
Due to the fermionic nature of the modes, all terms of higher than linear order in the exponentials vanish

e^{-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}}e^{-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}}=(1-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n})(1-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n})=1-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}+b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}.

(9.B.15)

The basic commutation relations
[L^{g}_{0},b_{n}]=-nb_{n}
,
[L^{g}_{0},c_{n}]=-nc_{n}
(see Appendix
10.B
) lead to

\displaystyle e^{-s(L_{0}^{g}+\tilde{L}_{0}^{g})}e^{-b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}}e^{-\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}}\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}=\big(1-e^{-2ns}b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}-

\displaystyle e^{-2ns}\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle e^{-4ns}b_{-n}\tilde{c}_{-n}\tilde{b}_{-n}c_{-n}\big)\ket{\downarrow\downarrow}

(9.B.16)

Plugging this back into (
9.B.13
) and similarly expanding
e^{b_{n}\tilde{c}_{n}}e^{\tilde{b}_{n}c_{n}}
leads to many terms, most of which vanish. The ones that do not can be simplified using
b_{n}c_{-n}=-c_{-n}b_{n}+1
. The result is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{g}_{\text{closed}}(s)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2sa^{g}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(1-2e^{-2ns}+e^{-4ns}\big)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2sa^{g}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(1-e^{-2ns}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=2e^{-2s\big(a^{g}-\frac{1}{12}\big)}\eta\big(is/\pi\big)^{2}.

(9.B.17)

Chapter 10
Ten-dimensional spinors

In this appendix, we build the ten-dimensional Clifford algebra in a Majorana-Weyl representation and derive Fierz identities of relevance to the discussion of the Ramond-Ramond vacuum of the RNS superstring. We also prove an identity for a product of gamma matrices that is used in Section
16
to find the spacetime equations of motion for the Ramond-Ramond field strengths.

Appendix 10.A
Clifford algebra and Fierz identities

In ten spacetime dimensions a Dirac spinor has
2^{10/2}=32
components, so the Dirac matrices are
32\times 32
. A Majorana representation for them with all matrices real is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}

\displaystyle=i\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{1}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{3}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{3}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{4}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{3}\otimes\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{5}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0}

(10.A.1)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{6}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{3}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{7}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{8}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{3}\otimes\sigma_{2}\otimes\sigma_{0}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{9}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0},

where

\displaystyle\sigma^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&1\\
1&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&-i\\
i&&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma^{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&&0\\
0&&-1\end{pmatrix}\,

(10.A.2)

are the Pauli matrices and
\sigma_{0}=-\mathbb{1}_{2\times 2}
is the timelike component of the Pauli vector
\sigma_{\mu}=(-\mathbb{1}_{2\times 2},\sigma^{i})
. From these one may form the generators of the full 10-dimensional Clifford algebra:

\big\{\mathbb{1}_{32\times 32}\,,\Gamma^{\mu_{1}},\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},\dots,\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}}\big\},

(10.A.3)

where

\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}=\Gamma^{[\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{n}]}.

(10.A.4)

Using the mixed product identity

(A_{1}\otimes B_{1}\otimes\dots)(A_{2}\otimes B_{2}\otimes\dots)\dots=(A_{1}A_{2}\dots)\otimes(B_{1}B_{2}\dots)\otimes\dots

(10.A.5)

it is easy to check that the chiral matrix is block-diagonal

\Gamma=\Gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}\Gamma^{2}\Gamma^{3}\Gamma^{4}\Gamma^{5}\Gamma^{6}\Gamma^{7}\Gamma^{8}\Gamma^{9}=\sigma_{3}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{16\times 16}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{1}_{16\times 16}&&0\\
0&&-\mathbb{1}_{16\times 16}\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.6)

which shows that this representation is actually Majorana-Weyl.

We denote each component of a Majorana spinor as
\varphi_{A}
. The Dirac matrices are then written as
(\Gamma^{\mu})_{A}{}^{B}
, so the basic anticommutator of the Clifford algebra reads

\{\Gamma^{\mu},\Gamma^{\nu}\}_{A}{}^{B}=(\Gamma^{\mu})_{A}{}^{C}(\Gamma^{\nu})_{C}{}^{B}+(\Gamma^{\nu})_{A}{}^{C}(\Gamma^{\mu})_{C}{}^{B}=2\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{A}^{B}.

(10.A.7)

When dealing with Majorana spinors the charge conjugation matrix
C
plays an important role. It is defined by the requirement that

(\Gamma^{\mu})^{\text{T}}=-C\,\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}.

(10.A.8)

If all Dirac matrices are real,
(\Gamma^{0})^{\text{T}}=-\Gamma^{0}
and
(\Gamma^{i})^{\text{T}}=\Gamma^{i}
, so the above condition is satisfied by
C=\Gamma^{0}
. This means that both
C\Gamma^{\mu}
and
\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}
are symmetric:

\displaystyle(C\Gamma^{\mu})^{\text{T}}=(\Gamma^{\mu})^{\text{T}}C^{\text{T}}=(-1)^{2}C\,\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}C=C\Gamma^{\mu},

(10.A.9)

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1})^{\text{T}}=-(C^{-1})^{\text{T}}C\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}=-C^{2}\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}=\Gamma^{\mu}C^{-1}.

(10.A.10)

Although
C
and
\Gamma^{0}
are the same component-wise, we write
\Gamma^{0}
with one lower and one upper index, while components of the charge conjugation matrix are written as
C^{AB}
, and those of the inverse
C^{-1}
are
C_{AB}
. From two Majorana spinors
\psi
and
\varphi
, a Lorentz scalar is built by contracting one with the Majorana conjugate of the other, defined as as
(\overline{\psi})^{A}\equiv\psi^{A}=\psi_{B}C^{BA}
.
77
77

77

For real spinors the Majorana conjugate coincides with the Dirac conjugate,
\overline{\psi}=\psi^{\text{T}}C=\psi^{\dagger}\Gamma^{0}
.
This scalar is

\overline{\psi}\varphi=\psi_{A}C^{AB}\varphi_{B}.

(10.A.11)

C^{AB}
and
C_{AB}
therefore play the role of a metric for spinor indices, and can be used to raise and lower them. Note however that the charge conjugation matrix is antisymmetric, so
C^{AB}\psi_{B}=-\psi_{B}C^{BA}
. We fix this ambiguity by demanding that index raising and lowering be done always with the upper index on the right and the lower index on the left:

\psi^{A}=\psi_{B}C^{BA}\,,\hskip 14.22636pt\psi_{A}=C_{AB}\psi^{B}.

(10.A.12)

Consistency of these two definitions requires
C_{BA}C^{BC}=\delta^{C}_{A}
, which simply expresses the fact that
C^{\text{T}}=C^{-1}
component-wise. Using these conventions we have that

\overline{\psi}\,\varphi=\psi_{A}C^{AB}\varphi_{B}=\psi^{A}C_{AB}\varphi^{B}=\psi^{A}\varphi_{A}=-\psi_{A}\varphi^{A}.

(10.A.13)

The normalized Clifford algebra

\hskip 22.76228pt\frac{1}{\sqrt{32}}\Big\{\delta^{B}_{A}\,,(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}})_{A}{}^{B},(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})_{A}{}^{B},\dots,(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}})_{A}{}^{B}\Big\}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with}\hskip 8.5359pt\mu_{1}<\mu_{2}<\dots<\mu_{10}

(10.A.14)

provides an orthogonal basis for the space of real
32\times 32
matrices
M_{A}{}^{B}
with respect to the inner product
(M,N)=\text{Tr}(M^{\text{T}}N)

[
95
]
.
78
78

78

This inner product induces a norm for real matrices which is a direct generalization of the usual real vector norm given by summing over the square of each component,
|v|^{2}=\sum_{i}(v^{i})^{2}
,

|M|^{2}=(M,M)=(M^{\text{T}})_{A}{}^{B}M_{B}{}^{A}=\sum_{A,B}(M_{B}{}^{A})^{2}.

(10.A.15)

The expansion of an arbitrary matrix in this basis is given by

M_{A}{}^{B}=\frac{1}{32}\sum_{n=0}^{10}\big(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}},M\big)\big(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}\big)_{A}{}^{B},\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(no sum)}

(10.A.16)

where
\mu_{i}<\mu_{i+1}
and we are not summing over the Lorentz indices. If all the
\mu_{i}
are distinct, we have that

\big(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{n}}\big)^{\text{T}}=\Gamma_{\mu_{n}}\dots\Gamma_{\mu_{1}}=(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\Gamma_{\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma_{\mu_{n}},

(10.A.17)

and can therefore rewrite the expansion as

M_{A}{}^{B}=\frac{1}{32}\sum_{n=0}^{10}\frac{1}{n!}(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\text{Tr}\big(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}M\big)\big(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}\big)_{A}{}^{B},

(10.A.18)

where this time we are using the Einstein summation convention for the Lorentz indices, which leads to an overcounting in the sum that is canceled by the factor of
1/n!
.

In ten dimensions the Hodge dual of an antisymmetric tensor
T_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}
of rank
n
is given by
79
79

79

We use
\epsilon_{0123456789}=-\epsilon^{0123456789}=1
.

[
96
]

(\ast\,T)_{\mu_{n+1}\dots\mu_{10}}=\frac{1}{(10-n)!}\epsilon_{\mu_{n+1}\dots\mu_{10}\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}},

(10.A.19)

which has rank
10-n
. Both carry the same information, since the Hodge star operator is invertible. One may use this fact to trade an antisymmetric tensor with more than five indices for one with less. This is implemented in the Clifford algebra with the chiral matrix. First, note that the rank 10 matrix is simply

\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}}=\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{10}}=-\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}}\Gamma,

(10.A.20)

where we assume with no loss of generality that all
\mu_{i}
are distinct. Let us momentarily suspend the Einstein summation convention, so that all Dirac matrices satisfy
\Gamma_{\mu}\Gamma^{\mu}=\mathbb{1}_{32\times 32}
. We may then write
\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{n}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{n}}\Gamma_{\mu_{n+1}}\Gamma^{\mu_{n+1}}\Gamma_{\mu_{n+2}}\Gamma^{\mu_{n+2}}\dots\Gamma_{\mu_{10}}\Gamma^{\mu_{10}}

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}\Gamma^{\mu_{n}}\Gamma^{\mu_{n+1}}\dots\Gamma^{\mu_{10}}\Gamma_{\mu_{n+1}}\dots\Gamma_{\mu_{10}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}\mu_{n+1}\dots\mu_{10}}\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{n+1}}\dots\Gamma_{\mu_{10}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(no sum)},

(10.A.21)

which upon restoring the summation convention becomes

\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}=(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\frac{1}{(10-n)!}\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}\mu_{n+1}\dots\mu_{10}}\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{n+1}\dots\mu_{10}},

(10.A.22)

with the factor of
1/(10-n)!
added to compensate the overcounting due to summing over Lorentz indices. Multiplying both sides by
\Gamma
gives

\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}=(-1)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(\ast\Gamma)^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}.

(10.A.23)

These formulas, together with the Levi-Civita contraction identity

\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}\nu_{n+1}\dots\nu_{10}}\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}\rho_{n+1}\dots\rho_{10}}=-n!(10-n)!\delta_{\nu_{n+1}}^{[\rho_{n+1}}\dots\delta_{\nu_{10}}^{\rho_{10}]},

(10.A.24)

allow the expansion of
M_{A}{}^{B}
to be written as

\displaystyle M_{A}{}^{B}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{32}\bigg[\text{Tr}(M)\delta_{A}^{B}+\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}}M)(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}})_{A}{}^{B}-\frac{1}{2!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}M)(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}M)(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}})_{A}{}^{B}+\frac{1}{4!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}M)(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}}M)(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}})_{A}{}^{B}+\frac{1}{4!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}M)(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}M)(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}})_{A}{}^{B}-\frac{1}{2!}\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}M)(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle-\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}}M)(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}})_{A}{}^{B}+\text{Tr}(\Gamma M)(\Gamma)_{A}{}^{B}\bigg].

(10.A.25)

The analogous expressions for different index structures can be easily obtained from this using the rules (
10.A.12
) for raising and lowering the indices. The most common application of expressions such as these is the derivation of Fierz identities, which follow from
M_{A}{}^{B}=\varphi_{A}\psi^{B}
. They are also useful for finding the most general form of supersymmetry algebras, for which one sets
M_{A}{}^{B}=\{Q_{A},Q^{B}\}
.

In even dimensions the Dirac representation is reducible. One may split the
A
index into
(\alpha,\dot{\alpha})
, where
\alpha
goes over the 16 components of a right-handed Weyl spinor and
\dot{\alpha}
goes over the 16 components of left-handed Weyl spinor,

\psi_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{\alpha}\\
\chi_{\dot{\alpha}}\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.26)

each one defined by its eigenvalue with respect to
\Gamma
,
+1
for right-handed,
-1
for left-handed. In the representation (
10.A.1
) the Dirac matrices are all block-antisymmetric:

(\Gamma^{\mu})_{A}{}^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma^{\mu})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
(\gamma^{\mu})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix}.

(10.A.27)

The
\gamma^{\mu}
are the ten-dimensional analogue of the Pauli matrices. The charge conjugation matrix similarly splits as

C^{AB}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\\
\mathcal{C}^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix},\hskip 14.22636ptC_{AB}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\\
\mathcal{C}_{\dot{\alpha}\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix}.

(10.A.28)

By taking products of (
10.A.27
) one finds that the form of the elements of the Clifford algebra change depending on whether the rank is even or odd:

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}&&0\\
0&&(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.29)

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.30)

where
\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}\equiv\gamma^{[\mu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\mu_{n}]}
. With these expressions one may plug

M_{A}{}^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}&0\end{pmatrix}

(10.A.31)

into (
10.A.25
) to find the expansion of
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}
in terms of antisymmetric tensors. The even rank coefficients
\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}}M)
all vanish, because

(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}}M)_{A}{}^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}&&0\\
0&&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\beta}}{}^{\beta}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\beta}}{}^{\beta}&0\end{pmatrix}

(10.A.32)

is traceless. For the odd rank ones we have

(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}M)_{A}{}^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\beta}}{}^{\beta}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\beta}}{}^{\beta}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.33)

so
\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}M)=\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}\mathcal{M})
. The dualized tensors are given by

\displaystyle(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{\alpha}^{\gamma}&0\\
0&-\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\gamma}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\gamma}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
-(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}&&0\end{pmatrix}.

(10.A.34)

This minus sign does not contribute to the trace, so
\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}M)=\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}\mathcal{M})
, but it does enter the expansion via
(\Gamma\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}=-(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}
, leading to twice the contribution of each odd rank tensor:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}}\mathcal{M})^{+}(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}\bigg].

(10.A.35)

The plus sign on
\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}\mathcal{M})^{+}
means that it is self-dual. In ten dimensions a five-form
T_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
has the same rank as its Hodge dual. Therefore, it can always be decomposed into a self-dual part
T^{+}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
, which satisfies
(\ast T)^{+}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}=T^{+}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
, and an anti self-dual part
T^{-}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
, satisfying
(\ast T)^{-}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}=-T^{-}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
,

T_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\ast\big)T_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}+\frac{1}{2}\big(1-\ast\big)T_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}=T^{+}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}+T^{-}_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}.

(10.A.36)

For
\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
one takes the dual by acting with
\Gamma
. The decomposition of
\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}
in terms of its self-dual and anti self-dual parts is therefore nothing but the decomposition into chirality eigenstates, and for the tensor
\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)
we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)^{+}+\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)^{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\big((\mathbb{1}_{32\times 32}+\Gamma)\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M\big)+\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\big((\mathbb{1}_{32\times 32}-\Gamma)\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M\big).

(10.A.37)

For
M_{A}{}^{B}
given by (
10.A.31
), only the self-dual part contributes:

\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)=\text{Tr}(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}M)^{+}=\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{5}}\mathcal{M})^{+}.

(10.A.38)

An analogous calculation shows that the anti self-dual part appears in
80
80

80

Whether the five-form in
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}
is the self-dual or anti self-dual one depends on the sign chosen in
\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}}=\pm\epsilon^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{10}}\Gamma
, which is a matter of convention.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}}\mathcal{M})^{-}(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\bigg].

(10.A.39)

Using the charge conjugation matrix to raise the first index of
\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\beta}
results in

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{3!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}})^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})^{+}(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}\mu_{5}})^{\alpha\beta}\bigg],

(10.A.40)

where
(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}})^{\alpha\beta}=(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}})_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\beta}\mathcal{C}^{\dot{\gamma}\alpha}=-\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\dot{\gamma}}(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}})_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\beta}
, and with the coefficients reexpressed as

(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\beta}}{}^{\alpha}=(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\mathcal{C}_{\dot{\beta}\gamma}\mathcal{M^{\gamma\alpha}}=\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M}).

(10.A.41)

Using instead

M_{A}{}^{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.42)

one finds

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}}M)_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\alpha}{}^{\gamma}&&0\\
0&&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\gamma}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\gamma}}\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.43)

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}}M)_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\gamma}}\\
(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\gamma}&&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n+1}})_{\alpha}{}^{\dot{\gamma}}\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\gamma}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

(10.A.44)

so in this case only the even rank tensors contribute. For the dual tensors one finds a minus sign in the coefficients
\text{Tr}(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}}M)=-\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}}\mathcal{M})
that cancels the one in
(\Gamma\Gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{A}{}^{B}=-(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{2n}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}
, leading to

\mathcal{M}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\text{Tr}(\mathcal{M})\delta_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\dot{\beta}}-\frac{1}{2!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}+\frac{1}{4!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}})_{\dot{\alpha}}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\bigg].

(10.A.45)

Raising one index gives

\mathcal{M}^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\text{Tr}(\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})\mathcal{C}^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}-\frac{1}{2!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}+\frac{1}{4!}\text{Tr}(\gamma_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathcal{M})(\gamma^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}})^{\alpha\dot{\beta}}\bigg].

(10.A.46)

Appendix 10.B
Proof of the gamma product identity

In Section
16
we make use of the identity

\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}=\gamma^{\mu\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}+n\eta^{\mu[\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{n}]}.

(10.B.1)

To prove it, start from

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{[\nu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1}\bigg(\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{[\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}]}-\gamma^{[\nu_{1}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\mu_{n}]}+\gamma^{[\nu_{1}\nu_{2}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{3}\dots\nu_{n}]}-\dots+(-1)^{n}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}\gamma_{\mu}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\gamma^{[\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{k}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}]}.

(10.B.2)

The anticommutator
\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=2\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}
can then be used to move
\gamma_{\mu}
to the left in each term:

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{k}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2(-1)^{k}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{k}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-2}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-2}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-2(-1)^{k-1}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-2}}\gamma^{\nu_{k}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2(-1)^{k}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{k}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k}}-2\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{n}}+2\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{2}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\nu_{3}\dots\nu_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\dots+2(-1)^{k}\delta_{\mu}^{\nu_{k}}\gamma^{\nu_{1}}\dots\gamma^{\nu_{k-1}}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{m}}.

(10.B.3)

Moving
\gamma_{\mu}
to the left side past all of the
k
matrices produces a total of
k
Kronecker delta terms. All of them in which the
\nu_{i}
indices are ordered in an even permutation of
(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n})
have a minus sign in front. All in which the permutation is odd have a plus sign. Antisymmetrizing with respect to all the
\nu_{i}
therefore gives

(-1)^{k}\gamma^{[\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{k}}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{k+1}\dots\nu_{n}]}=\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}-2k\delta_{\mu}^{[\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{n}]}.

(10.B.4)

We find then

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{[\nu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}]}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{n}}-\frac{1}{n+1}\delta_{\mu}^{[\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{n}]}\sum_{k=0}^{n}k

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu\dots\nu_{n}}-n\delta_{\mu}^{[\nu_{1}}\gamma^{\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{n}]},

(10.B.5)

which becomes (
10.B.1
) upon raising the
\mu
index.
